### Chapter 6.6 — Practical Strategies for Interfaith and Intercultural Engagement

#### Learning objectives
*   Apply active listening and empathetic inquiry techniques to effectively understand diverse spiritual and cultural needs.
*   Identify ethical boundaries in interfaith and intercultural spiritual care, particularly regarding proselytization.
*   Develop strategies for adapting spiritual care practices (e.g., prayer, ritual, silence) to be culturally and religiously congruent.
*   Utilize resources and collaborate with community leaders to enhance support for patients from diverse backgrounds.

#### Detailed lesson content
Having cultivated self-awareness and a foundational understanding of diverse traditions, chaplains must now translate this knowledge into practical, actionable strategies for interfaith and intercultural engagement. The goal is not merely to avoid offense, but to actively create a space where individuals feel seen, heard, and genuinely supported in their own spiritual framework.

At the heart of effective interfaith and intercultural care lies **active listening** and **empathetic inquiry**. This means listening not just to the words, but to the underlying emotions, values, and worldviews. Empathetic inquiry involves asking open-ended questions that invite individuals to share their story and their spiritual landscape, without imposing the chaplain's own assumptions. Instead of asking, "Do you want me to pray for you?" (which assumes a specific form of prayer and a belief in a personal God), a chaplain might ask, "What spiritual practices or beliefs bring you comfort or strength in times like these?" or "How does your faith tradition help you make sense of what you're experiencing?" This approach respects the individual's unique path and empowers them to articulate their needs. Phrases like "Tell me more about that" or "Help me understand what that means for you" are invaluable tools for building rapport and gaining insight.

**Ethical boundaries** are paramount, especially concerning **proselytization**. A chaplain's role is to provide spiritual care within the patient's expressed tradition or spiritual framework, not to convert them to another faith. This means refraining from imposing one's own religious beliefs, making judgmental statements about other faiths, or using the vulnerability of illness as an opportunity for evangelism. The line between sharing one's own spiritual resources (if appropriate and requested) and proselytizing can be subtle, but the key is always the patient's agency and expressed need. If a patient asks about the chaplain's faith, a brief, respectful answer is appropriate, but the focus must immediately return to the patient's spiritual journey. A chaplain might say, "As a chaplain, my role is to support you in your own spiritual journey, whatever that may be. While I am [mention your faith if relevant and brief], my primary focus is on what brings *you* comfort and meaning."

**Adapting spiritual care practices** requires creativity and flexibility.
*   **Prayer:** If a patient desires prayer, inquire about their preferred language, style, and content. Offer to pray *with* them in a way that resonates with their tradition, or offer a silent prayer of presence and intention. For example, a chaplain might offer, "Would you like me to offer a prayer that reflects your [faith tradition], or perhaps a moment of silent reflection?"
*   **Rituals:** Many traditions have specific rituals for healing, blessing, or transition. Chaplains should be open to facilitating these where appropriate and safe, or connecting patients with their specific religious leaders. This could involve finding a quiet space for meditation, facilitating smudging ceremonies (with appropriate safety protocols), or helping a family arrange for specific end-of-life rites.
*   **Sacred Texts/Music:** Offer to read from the patient's sacred text (if available and desired) or play music that holds spiritual significance for them.
*   **Silence:** Sometimes, the most profound spiritual care is offered in respectful silence, simply being present with the individual's suffering or peace. This is particularly relevant in traditions that emphasize contemplation or where direct verbal expression of distress is less common.

**Resource utilization and collaboration** are crucial for comprehensive care. No chaplain can be an expert in every religion or culture. Therefore, knowing when and how to connect patients with their specific religious leaders, cultural community organizations, or interpreters is vital. Building a network of interfaith contacts and cultural liaisons in the community is an invaluable asset. For instance, if a patient requires a specific ritual that the chaplain cannot perform, the chaplain's role is to facilitate connection with a qualified leader from that tradition. Utilizing professional interpreters for language barriers (rather than relying on family members, which can compromise privacy and accuracy) is also a critical aspect of culturally competent communication. This collaborative approach ensures that patients receive the most authentic and congruent spiritual support possible.

By combining empathetic presence, ethical boundaries, adaptable practices, and effective collaboration, chaplains can navigate the rich tapestry of human spirituality with grace and effectiveness, truly embodying the spirit of interfaith and intercultural competence.

#### Key concepts
*   **Active Listening:** A communication technique requiring the listener to fully concentrate, understand, respond, and remember what is being said, including non-verbal cues.
*   **Empathetic Inquiry:** Asking open-ended questions with genuine curiosity and empathy to understand another person's perspective, feelings, and spiritual needs.
*   **Proselytization:** The act of attempting to convert someone from one religion, belief, or opinion to another.
*   **Ethical Boundaries:** Professional limits and guidelines that ensure the safety, respect, and well-being of those receiving care, preventing exploitation or harm.
*   **Cultural Congruence:** Providing care that is consistent with the patient's cultural beliefs, values, and practices.
*   **Resource Utilization:** The effective use of available community, religious, and professional resources to support patient care.

#### Hands-on activity
**Developing Empathetic Inquiry Questions**
For each of the following patient scenarios, formulate two open-ended, empathetic inquiry questions that a chaplain could use to explore their spiritual needs without making assumptions or imposing a specific religious framework.

1.  **Scenario 1:** A young adult patient, who identifies as "spiritual but not religious," is struggling with a recent diagnosis of chronic illness.
    *   Question 1:
    *   Question 2:

2.  **Scenario 2:** An elderly patient from a Hindu background is nearing end-of-life, and their family is present, visibly distressed.
    *   Question 1:
    *   Question 2:

3.  **Scenario 3:** A patient from a secular background expresses anger and frustration with their situation, stating, "There's no point to any of this."
    *   Question 1:
    *   Question 2:

*Model Responses (for instructor guidance):*
1.  **Scenario 1:** "Spiritual but not religious"
    *   "What does 'spiritual' mean to you, and how does that inform how you're coping with this illness?"
    *   "Are there any particular practices, beliefs, or sources of strength that you draw upon when facing difficult times?"
2.  **Scenario 2:** Elderly Hindu patient nearing end-of-life
    *   "How does your Hindu faith help you and your family understand this transition, and what practices bring you comfort right now?"
    *   "Is there anything specific I can do to support your spiritual well-being or help your family observe important rituals?"
3.  **Scenario 3:** Secular patient expressing anger/frustration
    *   "It sounds like you're carrying a heavy burden right now. Can you tell me more about what feels pointless?"
    *   "What has given you a sense of purpose or meaning in the past, and how might we explore that now?"

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A chaplain is visiting a patient who identifies as Agnostic and expresses discomfort with traditional religious language. The chaplain's personal faith is deeply rooted in Christian prayer. What is the most appropriate and ethical way for the chaplain to offer spiritual support?
    a) Offer a Christian prayer, explaining that it's a universal way to connect with a higher power.
    b) Avoid any mention of spirituality or prayer, focusing only on practical concerns.
    c) Ask the patient, "What brings you peace or meaning?" and offer to sit in silence, listen, or engage in a reflective conversation about their values.
    d) Suggest they read a book on comparative religions to find a spiritual path that suits them.

    **Correct Answer:** c) Ask the patient, "What brings you peace or meaning?" and offer to sit in silence, listen, or engage in a reflective conversation about their values.
    **Rationale:** Option (c) demonstrates respect for the patient's non-religious stance, uses empathetic inquiry, and offers support congruent with their expressed comfort level. Option (a) is proselytizing. Option (b) is avoidant. Option (d) is inappropriate and dismissive of the patient's current needs.

2.  **Question:** Describe two specific strategies a chaplain can employ to adapt their spiritual care to be culturally and religiously congruent for a patient from a non-Western background who values family collective decision-making.

    **Model Response:**
    1.  **Engage the Family Respectfully:** Instead of focusing solely on the individual patient for decision-making or spiritual conversations, the chaplain should respectfully include key family members in discussions, if desired by the patient and culturally appropriate. This might involve greeting and acknowledging the family first, asking permission to speak with the patient, and framing questions to the patient in a way that includes the family's perspective (e.g., "How does your family's wisdom guide you in this situation?"). This respects the collective decision-making process and builds trust with the entire support system.
    2.  **Inquire about Preferred Rituals/Practices:** The chaplain should use empathetic inquiry to ask about any specific family or cultural rituals, prayers, or practices that are important during illness or end-of-life. For example, "Are there any family traditions or spiritual practices that bring comfort or are important to observe at this time?" This opens the door for the chaplain to facilitate these practices, connect with community leaders if needed, or simply be a respectful presence, ensuring that care aligns with the family's values and beliefs rather than imposing unfamiliar ones.

### Chapter 6.7 — Case Studies and Advanced Applications in Diverse Settings

#### Learning objectives
*   Analyze complex interfaith and intercultural spiritual care scenarios in various settings (e.g., healthcare, military, 
*   Evaluate the role of interdisciplinary collaboration and resource utilization in advanced diverse spiritual care.
*   Develop a structured approach to ethical decision-making when cultural or religious values conflict with institutional policies or medical recommendations.

#### Detailed lesson content
The true test of interfaith and intercultural competence lies in its application to complex, real-world scenarios. In diverse settings such as hospitals, military units, *healthcare setting** scenario: A patient from a deeply conservative religious tradition refuses a life-saving blood transfusion, citing religious objections. The medical team is ethically bound to preserve life, while the patient's autonomy and religious freedom are also paramount. Here, the chaplain's role is not to persuade the patient to accept the transfusion or to dismiss their beliefs. Instead, it is to:
1.  **Ensure Informed Consent:** Verify that the patient fully understands the medical implications of their refusal, including potential outcomes, without coercion.
2.  **Explore Alternatives:** Are there any religiously permissible alternatives to the transfusion? Can the medical team offer alternatives that align with the patient's faith (e.g., bloodless medicine techniques)?
3.  **Facilitate Dialogue:** Bridge communication between the medical team and the patient/family, helping each understand the other's perspective and ethical framework.
4.  **Support Spiritual Distress:** Address the spiritual and emotional distress the patient may be experiencing due to this conflict, offering presence, prayer (if desired and congruent), or connection to their religious leaders for guidance.
This situation highlights the ethical principle of **respect for autonomy** balanced with the chaplain's role in advocating for the patient's spiritual integrity.

In a **military setting**, chaplains often serve diverse personnel from various backgrounds, sometimes deployed in culturally sensitive regions. A chaplain might encounter a service member struggling with moral injury after an event that conflicts with their spiritual or ethical code. For example, a Buddhist soldier might struggle with the violence inherent in combat. The chaplain's role here is to:
1.  **Create a Safe Space:** Provide a confidential and non-judgmental environment for the service member to process their experiences.
2.  **Connect to Spiritual Resources:** Help the service member access spiritual resources within their own tradition that address concepts of suffering, forgiveness, or peace, or connect them with a Buddhist spiritual advisor.
3.  **Facilitate Meaning-Making:** Assist the service member in finding meaning or reconciliation within their experience, respecting their unique moral and spiritual framework.
4.  **Interdisciplinary Collaboration:** Work with mental health professionals to ensure comprehensive support for moral injury.
The challenge here is navigating the intersection of military duty, individual conscience, and diverse spiritual responses to trauma, requiring a chaplain to be both spiritually grounded and culturally agile.

In a **, chaplains serve a population often marked by trauma, marginalization, and diverse spiritual needs, sometimes including non-traditional or emergent spiritual paths. A chaplain might encounter an incarcerated individual who has converted to a new, less common spiritual tradition while in prison and is requesting specific dietary accommodations or ritual items that are not standard. The chaplain's approach should involve:
1.  **Respectful Inquiry:** Understand the depth and sincerity of their conversion and the significance of their requests.
2.  **Advocacy within Policy:** Advocate for the individual's religious freedom within the bounds of institutional safety and security policies. This may involve researching the tradition, consulting with experts, and presenting a case to administration.
3.  **Education and Bridge-Building:** Educate *Ethical Scrutiny:** Discern genuine spiritual needs from manipulative behaviors, a common challenge in *university setting**, chaplains support students from across the globe, often grappling with identity formation, academic stress, and cultural adjustment. A chaplain might encounter an international student experiencing profound homesickness and spiritual isolation, whose traditional coping mechanisms are unavailable. The chaplain's role is to:
1.  **Cultural Bridging:** Help the student connect with cultural or religious communities on campus or in the local area.
2.  **Adaptative Spiritual Care:** Offer spiritual care that is sensitive to their cultural background, perhaps through storytelling, shared meals, or facilitating connection with family abroad.
3.  **Holistic Support:** Collaborate with university counseling services, international student offices, and academic advisors to address the student's multifaceted needs.
4.  **Preventing Stereotypes:** Avoid assuming the student's spiritual needs based on their country of origin; always inquire about their individual practices and beliefs.
These scenarios underscore the need for chaplains to move beyond theoretical knowledge to dynamic, context-specific application, always guided by ethical principles and a commitment to the spiritual well-being of the individual. The ability to engage in **interdisciplinary collaboration** and to navigate potential conflicts between diverse values and institutional norms is a hallmark of advanced interfaith and intercultural chaplaincy.

#### Key concepts
*   **Ethical Decision-Making Model:** A structured framework for analyzing ethical dilemmas, considering principles, values, stakeholders, and potential outcomes.
*   **Moral Injury:** The psychological, social, and spiritual impact of perpetrating, failing to prevent, bearing witness to, or learning about acts that transgress deeply held moral beliefs and expectations.
*   **Interdisciplinary Collaboration:** Working effectively with professionals from various disciplines (e.g., medical staff, social workers, mental health professionals, community leaders) to provide holistic care.
*   **Advocacy:** The act of supporting or pleading for a cause or proposal, often on behalf of a patient or group.
*   **Autonomy:** The right or condition of self-government, particularly in healthcare, the patient's right to make decisions about their own medical care.
*   **Cultural Adjustment:** The process of adapting to a new or different culture, often involving challenges and growth.

#### Hands-on activity
**Ethical Dilemma Analysis: The Dietary Conflict**
*Scenario:* You are a chaplain in a hospital. A patient, Mr. Sharma, is a devout Hindu who requires a specific therapeutic diet (low-sodium, low-fat) due to a heart condition. However, Mr. Sharma's family insists on bringing him home-cooked meals, which are culturally significant and believed to aid healing, but often do not adhere to the prescribed dietary restrictions. The medical team is concerned about the patient's health, and the family is distressed by the hospital's "lack of respect" for their traditions. Mr. Sharma himself is torn between his family's wishes and the medical advice.

1.  **Identify Key Stakeholders and Their Values:** Who are the main parties involved, and what are their primary concerns or values?
2.  **Identify the Core Conflict:** What is the central ethical and cultural dilemma in this scenario?
3.  **Chaplain's Role and Initial Steps:** What is your primary role as a chaplain here, and what are the first 2-3 steps you would take to address this situation?
4.  **Propose a Resolution Strategy:** Outline a strategy that attempts to balance cultural sensitivity, medical necessity, and patient/family well-being.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** In a military setting, a chaplain learns that a service member from a non-Western cultural background is experiencing severe guilt and shame after a combat incident, but is unwilling to discuss it with mental health professionals due to cultural stigma. What is the chaplain's most appropriate and effective initial response?
    a) Immediately refer the service member to mental health services, emphasizing the importance of professional help.
    b) Privately offer a space for the service member to share their experience, validate their feelings of guilt and shame within their cultural context, and explore culturally congruent spiritual coping mechanisms.
    c) Advise the service member that their feelings are normal in combat and they should try to move past them.
    d) Contact the service member's commanding officer to report their distress and reluctance to seek help.

    **Correct Answer:** b) Privately offer a space for the service member to share their experience, validate their feelings of guilt and shame within their cultural context, and explore culturally congruent spiritual coping mechanisms.
    **Rationale:** Option (b) respects the service member's cultural reluctance, provides a safe space, validates their experience, and seeks to offer support within their cultural framework, which is crucial for building trust before any potential referral to mental health. Options (a) and (d) could further alienate the service member. Option (c) is dismissive.

2.  **Question:** Using the "Dietary Conflict" case study from the Hands-on Activity, articulate how you would employ interdisciplinary collaboration and ethical decision-making to work towards a resolution that respects both Mr. Sharma's cultural values and his medical needs.

    **Model Response:**
    In the "Dietary Conflict" scenario, I would employ interdisciplinary collaboration and ethical decision-making as follows:

    **Interdisciplinary Collaboration:**
    1.  **Engage the Medical Team:** I would first meet with the medical team (physician, dietitian, nurses) to understand the precise medical necessity of the diet, the risks of non-compliance, and any flexibility in the dietary plan. I would share my understanding of the family's cultural values regarding food and healing.
    2.  **Engage the Family:** I would meet with Mr. Sharma and his family, acknowledging their distress and the importance of their cultural practices. I would use empathetic inquiry to understand their specific concerns about hospital food and their beliefs about the healing power of home-cooked meals. I would also gently explain the medical team's concerns in a culturally sensitive manner.
    3.  **Facilitate a Joint Meeting (if appropriate):** If trust is established, I would suggest a facilitated conversation between the medical team and the family. My role would be to translate not just language, but also cultural and medical perspectives, ensuring everyone feels heard and understood.

    **Ethical Decision-Making:**
    1.  **Identify Ethical Principles:** The core principles in tension are patient autonomy (Mr. Sharma's choice), beneficence (the medical team's duty to do good by prescribing a therapeutic diet), and cultural respect (the family's values).
    2.  **Explore Creative Solutions:** With the medical team, I would explore if there are *any* aspects of the home-cooked meals that could be adapted to meet dietary restrictions (e.g., using specific ingredients, reducing salt/fat in certain dishes). Could the family prepare *some* approved items? Could the dietitian provide recipes or guidance for culturally appropriate low-sodium/low-fat dishes?
    3.  **Patient-Centered Compromise:** The ultimate goal is to find a solution that Mr. Sharma can adhere to, respecting his autonomy while safeguarding his health. This might involve a phased approach, limited approved home-cooked items, or a blend of hospital and family food. The chaplain's role is to help Mr. Sharma articulate his priorities and support him in making an informed decision that he can live with, even if it's a compromise.
    4.  **Spiritual Support:** Throughout this process, I would provide spiritual support to Mr. Sharma and his family, acknowledging the spiritual distress caused by this conflict between tradition and health, and helping them find peace and meaning within the chosen path.

    This collaborative and ethical approach aims to move beyond a win-lose scenario, seeking a culturally sensitive and medically sound compromise that respects the dignity and well-being of all involved.

## Module 7: Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE) Methodologies

**Module Goal:** To equip learners with a comprehensive understanding of Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE) methodologies, enabling them to engage in rigorous self-reflection, theological integration, and peer/supervisory processes essential for professional growth in spiritual care.

### Chapter 7.1 — Introduction to Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE)

#### Learning objectives
*   Articulate the historical origins and foundational principles of Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE).
*   Explain the unique pedagogical approach of CPE, emphasizing experiential learning and action-reflection.
*   Identify the core components and goals of a standard CPE unit.
*   Differentiate CPE from other forms of spiritual education or counseling training.
*   Recognize the significance of CPE as a prerequisite for professional chaplaincy certification.

#### Detailed lesson content
Welcome to Module 7, where we embark on a deep dive into Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE), a cornerstone of professional chaplaincy training. CPE is far more than just another course; it is an intensive, immersive, and transformative educational process designed to develop chaplains who are not only skilled but also deeply self-aware and ethically grounded. At its heart, CPE is an experiential learning model, meaning that you learn by doing, by reflecting on your actions, and by integrating new insights into future practice. This methodology distinguishes it significantly from traditional academic study, placing the chaplain's direct engagement with individuals in crisis, illness, or spiritual distress at the center of the learning process.

The origins of CPE can be traced back to the pioneering work of Anton T. Boisen in the 1920s. Boisen, a Presbyterian minister who experienced his own profound mental health crises, recognized that traditional theological education often left clergy unprepared for the complexities of human suffering and the real-world demands of pastoral care. He believed that ministers needed to learn directly from "living human documents"—the patients themselves—in clinical settings. Boisen established his first training program at Worcester State Hospital in Massachusetts, where students observed and engaged with patients, then reflected on these encounters with supervisors and peers. His revolutionary insight was that the "disturbed mind" could be a "textbook of living human documents," and that by studying these "texts," one could gain profound insights into both human nature and one's own spiritual and psychological dynamics. This historical context is crucial, as it underscores CPE's commitment to empirical observation, self-examination, and the integration of psychological and theological understanding.

A standard CPE unit typically involves a significant number of clinical hours, often in a hospital, hospice, or * as a caregiver, what your biases might be, what your strengths are, and how your own spiritual journey informs your practice. The process often involves confronting one's own vulnerabilities, anxieties, and theological assumptions, making it a profoundly challenging yet ultimately rewarding journey of self-discovery.

One of the common misconceptions about CPE is that it is simply "on-the-job training" or an extended internship. While it certainly involves practical experience, CPE is a highly structured educational methodology rooted in adult learning theories, particularly Kolb's Experiential Learning Cycle, which we will explore in the next chapter. It systematically moves learners through cycles of concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualization, and active experimentation. Another pitfall is to view CPE solely as a requirement for certification, rather than an opportunity for deep personal and professional transformation. Approaching CPE with a mindset of mere compliance rather than genuine curiosity and openness to growth will diminish its impact significantly. The true value of CPE lies in its capacity to refine one's spiritual care skills, deepen theological understanding through lived experience, and foster a robust professional identity capable of navigating the complex ethical and emotional landscapes of chaplaincy. For this reason, most professional chaplaincy organizations, such as the Association of Professional Chaplains (APC) and Neshama: Association of Jewish Chaplains (NAJC), require multiple units of CPE for certification, recognizing its indispensable role in preparing chaplains for competent and compassionate ministry.

#### Key concepts
*   **Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE):** An interfaith professional education for ministry that brings theological students and ministers into supervised encounters with people in crisis.
*   **Anton T. Boisen:** The founder of the CPE movement, who emphasized learning from "living human documents" in clinical settings.
*   **Experiential Learning:** A pedagogical approach where learning occurs through direct experience, followed by reflection, conceptualization, and application.
*   **Living Human Documents:** Boisen's term for patients and their experiences, which serve as the primary "textbook" for learning in CPE.
*   **Action-Reflection Model:** The core process in CPE where students engage in pastoral care (action), then reflect on their experiences to gain insight and improve practice.
*   **CPE Unit:** A structured period of intense clinical ministry, supervision, and didactic learning, typically lasting 3-4 months full-time or longer part-time.

#### Hands-on activity
**Reflecting on Initial Perceptions of CPE**
Consider what you already know or believe about Clinical Pastoral Education (CPE). Write a short reflection (250-300 words) addressing the following prompts:
1.  What are your initial assumptions or expectations about what CPE entails?
2.  Based on the historical context of Anton Boisen, how might CPE challenge traditional theological education?
3.  What potential benefits or challenges do you anticipate from an experiential learning model in a spiritual care context?
4.  How might the concept of "living human documents" influence your approach to learning about spiritual care?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** Anton T. Boisen's foundational insight for CPE was that ministers should learn from "living human documents." Explain what Boisen meant by this phrase and how it shaped the unique pedagogical approach of CPE compared to traditional theological education.
    *   **Model Response:** Boisen's concept of "living human documents" refers to patients and individuals experiencing crisis, illness, or spiritual distress in clinical settings. He believed that these individuals, through their lived experiences and narratives, offered a rich "textbook" for ministers to study and learn from, far beyond what purely academic theological study could provide. This insight led to CPE's unique experiential learning approach, where students directly engage with people in need, then reflect deeply on these encounters. Unlike traditional theological education, which often focuses on theoretical knowledge and abstract concepts, CPE emphasizes direct observation, personal engagement, and the integration of psychological and theological understanding derived from real-world pastoral encounters, fostering empathy, self-awareness, and practical skills.

2.  **Question:** A common misconception about CPE is that it is merely an extended internship. How would you explain the fundamental difference between CPE's experiential learning model and a typical internship, highlighting at least two distinct features of CPE?
    *   **Model Response:** While CPE involves practical experience like an internship, it fundamentally differs due to its highly structured educational methodology and intense focus on self-reflection and personal growth. First, CPE utilizes a systematic action-reflection cycle, often rooted in models like Kolb's, where every clinical encounter is rigorously processed through written reports (verbatims), peer group discussion, and individual supervision, aiming for deep learning and behavioral change, not just task completion. Second, CPE places significant emphasis on the chaplain's "self as instrument," exploring the chaplain's own spiritual, psychological, and theological dynamics as they impact caregiving. This level of self-awareness and personal integration, guided by expert supervision, goes far beyond the typical skill acquisition or observation goals of an internship, aiming for a transformation of professional identity.

Use animated text overlays to define "living human documents" and "experiential learning." Include a segment with a contemporary chaplain briefly explaining the personal impact of CPE on their practice. The visual style should be scholarly but engaging, with clear graphics illustrating the core components of a CPE unit. End with a reflection prompt on the screen for 30 seconds.
